• Looking at the core business, 3Q26 revenue reached TWD52.7bn (up 23% QoQ, up 
55% YoY), with gross margin expanding 4.0ppt QoQ to 28.8% and operating margin 
rising 4.5ppt QoQ to 20.0%, supported by improving profitability in ABF substrates and 
HDI. With stronger operating leverage, operating profit surged 58% QoQ to 
TWD10.53bn. We believe the gross-margin and operating-margin performance was 
broadly in line with recent buy-side expectations for modestly improving profitability in 
ABF substrates and HDI, given that ABF margin was already high at c.40% in 2Q26, 
alongside a higher revenue contribution from a large end customer. We estimate ABF 
substrate margin at c.42-44% in 3Q26 (vs. c.40% in 2Q26).

• Business outlook. While the company does not provide numerical guidance, revenue 
and gross margin should continue to improve in 4Q26 amid the current substrate supply 
shortage. In addition, the company is aggressively expanding capacity at its Guangfu 
and Yangmei plants over 2027-29, with roughly c.30% annual ABF capacity expansion, 
which should become a key growth driver for Unimicron. Given Unimicron’s leading 
position across most CSP AI ASIC projects, Nvidia GPUs, and EMIB-T, we believe the 
company should continue to benefit from a favourable pricing environment amid the 
current supply-demand dynamics and ongoing specification upgrades, including larger 
substrate body sizes and technology migration.

What we recommend: 
We have a Buy (1) rating on Unimicron with a 12M TP of TWD1,500, based on a target 
PER of 35x applied to our 1-year-forward EPS forecast. Key downside risk: less-than-
expected demand.

市场研究部 2026年 10月 14日
事件
10月 14日，美国劳工部发布，9月美国 CPI环比上涨 0.6%，
同比上涨 3.7%；季调后，环比上涨 0.6%，同比上涨 3.7%。
解读
一、9 月美国 CPI 环比涨幅扩大，能源价格上涨是主要原因。
9 月美国 CPI 环比上涨 0.6%，比 8 月提高 0.3 个百分点，季
调后，环比上涨 0.6%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点。
其中，食品价格环比涨 0.1%，与 8 月持平；能源价格环比上
涨 3.7%，比 8 月提高 2.4 个百分点；核心 CPI 环比上涨 0.4%，
比 8月提高 0.1个百分点。
油价环比上涨。9月国际油价（纽约原油价格）均价 92.70 美
元/桶，比 8月均价（78.33美元/桶美元）上涨 18.35%；布伦特原
油价格平均为 97.01 美元/桶，比 8 月均价（82.72 美元/桶美元）
上涨 17.28%。
8大类商品和服务价格环比普涨，与 8月相比，环比涨幅 1降
1平 6升：
其中，交通运输价格环比上涨 0.5%，比 8 月提高 1.1 个百分

点，
服装价格环比上涨 1.7%，比 8月提高 1.2个百分点。
教育与通信价格环比上涨 0.4%，比 8月回落 1.5个百分点，
食品与饮料价格环比上涨 0.1%，与 8月持平，
医疗保健价格环比上涨 0.1%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点，
其它商品和服务价格环比上涨 0.4%，比 8 月提高 0.3 个百分
点，
住宅价格环比上涨 0.3%，比 8月提高 0.1个百分点，
娱乐价格环比上涨 0.5，比 8月提高 0.5个百分点。
二、9 月美国 CPI 同比涨幅扩大，核心 CPI 同比小幅提高
9 月，美国 CPI 同比上涨 3.7%，比 8 月提高 0.3 个百分点，
自 3月以来，连续 7个月高于 3%。
宏观研究

其中，食品价格同比上涨 2.5%，比 8 月回落 0.2 个百分点；
能源价格同比上涨 20.4%，比 8月提高 4.1个百分点；核心 CPI同
比上涨 2.6%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点。
8 大类商品和服务价格同比普涨，与 8 月同比涨幅相比，4降
4升。其中，
住宅价格同比上涨 3.3%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点；
服装价格同比上涨 3.4%，比 8月回落 0.2个百分点；
交通运输价格同比上涨 7.9%，比 8月提高 1.7个百分点；
其它商品及服务价格同比上涨 4.1%，比 8 月回落 0.1 个百分
点。
教育与通信价格同比上涨 2.3%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点；
医疗保健价格同比上涨 1.5%，比 8月回落 0.1个百分点；
娱乐价格同比上涨 2.8%，比 8月提高 0.1个百分点；
食品饮料价格同比上涨 2.4%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点。
9月，美国住所租金（非季调）环比上涨 0.3%，与 8月相同，
同比上涨 3.2%，比 8月提高 0.2个百分点。

9 月，美国服务（非季调）价格环比上涨 0.3%，比 8 月提高
0.1个百分点，同比上涨 3.2%，比 8月上升 0.1个百分点。
可见，9月除了能源价格同比上涨 20.4%，比 8月提高 4.1个
百分点，波动较大，并带动交通运输价格涨幅波动较大外，其余
消费价格同比波动 0.1~0.2个百分点，比较小。
三、9 月 CPI 数据不支持美联储加息决策。
9月美国 CPI 同比受能源价格上涨影响而涨幅小幅扩大，是在
中东冲突再度发酵诱使油价大涨情况发生的，核心 CPI、居住价格
和服务价格同比均比较平稳。8 月美核心 CPI 同比上涨 2.4%，离
2%的政策目标非常近。9 月 16 日，美联储加息 25 个基点，比较
牵强，即便 9月 CPI同比涨幅小幅扩大，也不支持再次加息。
美联储降息与否不影响中国央行货币政策，央行货币政策空间
仍然较大。
投资建议
1、9 月美国 CPI 同比主要受能源价格影响而小幅扩大，核心
CPI比较平稳。
2、9月美国 CPI数据不支持美联储加息决策。
3、美联储利率政策不会对中国货币政策构成制约，央行货币

政策空间仍然较大。
投 风险提示
1、地缘政治恶化超预期；2、欧美经济衰退超预期。

### Page 1

(034020) 
두산에너빌리티 
2026.10.30

에너지/인프라/배터리 황성현, Ph.D. 
6878 / tjdgus2009@eugenefn.com

펀더멘털은 견고, 노이즈로 인한 일시적 주가 하락 
 투자의견 BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 80,000원(하향) 
 현재주가 56,700 원(10/29)

 [투자의견] 당사는 두산에너빌리티에 대해 목표주가를 80,000원(2027년 
BPS의 6.2배)으로 27% 하향하며, 투자의견 BUY를 유지한다. 실적은 견조
한 회복을 보이고 있으나, 최근 GE Vernova(GEV) 등 글로벌 AI 및 에너지 
밸류체인 Peer 주가 조정을 반영해 멀티플을 하향 조정했다. 체코 시공 계
약 등 일부 노이즈에 의해 주가가 급락한 만큼 매수 기회라고 판단한다.  
 [3Q26 Review]  매출액 4.4조원(-5.8%qoq, +14.7%yoy), 영업이익 
2,612억원(-16.9%qoq, +90.6%yoy)을 기록해 당사 추정에 부합하는 무
난한 실적을 기록했다. 에너빌리티 부문은 매출액 1.8조원, 영업이익 1,211
억원(영업이익률 6.6%, +2.3%p qoq)을 기록하며 원자력·가스터빈 중심의 
고수익 사업 믹스 개선으로 분기 수익성이 재차 개선됐다. 당기순이익은 
149억원(-93.4%qoq)에 그쳤으나, 원달러 환율 급락으로 발생한 환손실 
반영 때문으로 이익 체력과는 무관하다고 판단한다. 3Q26 미국 빅테크 데
이터센터향 가스터빈 첫 출하를 달성했으며, 수주잔고는 에너빌리티 부문 
기준 26.6조원(+70.9%yoy)으로 사상 최대치를 경신했다. 
 [4Q26 Preview] 매출액 4.8조원, 영업이익 2,640억원으로 유사한 실적 
흐름을 전망한다. 4Q 가스터빈 인도와 체코 두코바니 원전 진행률 반영으로 
에너빌리티 부문 외형 성장이 기대된다. 3Q26 별도 기준 영업이익률이 
8.5%까지 개선된 만큼 에너빌리티 부문의 분기 실적 개선 추세는 유효하다
고 판단한다. 연간 수주 목표 13.3조원 달성을 위한 잔여 수주(3.3조원 +α)
가 예정되어 있으며, 대형원전을 제외하더라도 가이던스 달성은 무난할 것
이라 판단한다. 세부적으로는 SMR 1조원(계약 조건 합의 완료 단계로 연내 
체결 가능), 신재생 7,000억원(해상풍력 기자재·EPC 2건 협의 완료 단계), 
해외 복합 EPC 5,000억원(국내 1건, 해외 2건)이 기대된다. 
 [가스터빈] 3Q26 누적 가스터빈 13기·스팀터빈 11기·LTSA 6건을 달성하
며 연간 10기 이상 수주·수행 역량이 안정적으로 유지되고 있다. 2030년 
슬롯 예약 물량의 본계약 전환은 2027년, 2031년 슬롯은 2028년으로 예
상되며, 수주잔고 증가가 기대된다. 가스터빈 수요 증가를 감안하면 추가 캐
파 증설 결정이 이르면 연내 이루어질 전망이다. 
 [원전] 10월 NuScale 1개 모듈 제작 계약을 체결했으며, PPA 지연 여부와 
무관하게 완결된 계약으로 실적에 반영될 전망이다. X-energy는 연내 기자
재 및 부품의 후속 계약을 추진 중이다. TerraPower 주기기 3종 중 2종 계
약이 완료되어 제작에 착수했으며, Rolls-Royce SMR·i-SMR 등 파트너십 
확대도 지속되고 있다. 이외에 대미투자 관련 기자재 발주는 미국 부지 확정 
및 한국 의회 승인 절차가 남아있어 내년 발주가 기대된다. 기존 웨스팅하우
스 AP1000 Vogtle 계약 대비 업무 범위 확대(원자로·증기발생기 외 추가 
품목) 가능성이 있어 긍정적으로 평가한다.

### Page 2

두산에너빌리티

2_ Eugene Research Center 
설명회 Q&A 
Q1. 4Q26 SMR 수주 목표 약 1 조원의 실현 가능성 
- 고객사와 GTC 등 주요 계약 조건에 대한 합의를 대부분 완료하고 계약 체결 대기 중 
- 연내 계약 가능성이 있어 수주 목표는 의미 있는 수준

Q2. NuScale 모듈 1 기 제작 계약의 PP A 지연 영향 
- 완결된 제작 계약으로 PP A 체결이 지연되더라도 해당 모듈의 제작 및 진행에는 문제없음

Q3. AP1000 장주기 기자재 선발주 후 인허가 지연 시 회계 처리 및 사업 영향 
- DOE·DOC 및 한국 정부 재원을 활용해 EPC 계약·착공 지연과 무관하게 기자재 제작 가능 
- 프로젝트 중단 시 웨스팅하우스의 다른 프로젝트로 기자재 전용 가능성 검토

Q4. 체코 원전 컨소시엄 변경 시 참여 및 사업 계획 
- 현지 지점 운영과 시공인력 상주로 시공 환경 자료 확보 
- 한수원과 초기 인허가·설계에 참여하고, 주기기 공급·설치 역량을 바탕으로 경쟁력 확보 기대

Q5. 체코 원전 EPC 사업의 수익성 확보 가능성 
- 한수원 가이드에 따라 수익성을 고려한 입찰 진행 예정

Q6. 웨스팅하우스 SPV 및 폴란드 AP1000 장주기 기자재 발주 진행 상황 
- DOE·DOC 프로젝트와 폴란드 AP1000 3기 기자재 조기 발주 협의 중 
- 연내 수주 여부는 불확실하나 조기 수주 추진

Q7. 3Q26 영업이익률 개선 요인 
- 원자력·가스터빈 중심의 사업 믹스 개선이 수익성에 기여 
- 제작 진행률과 마일스톤에 따라 이익 인식

### Page 3

두산에너빌리티

Eugene Research Center _3 
Q8. 4Q26 복합화력 수주 목표 5,000억원의 달성 가능성 
- 국내 1 건, 해외 2 건 협의 중이며 국내 프로젝트의 수주 가능성이 높은 상황 
- 대미 투자 물량은 기존 2031년까지의 예약계약 납기와 비교해 검토 중

Q9. 3Q26 에너빌리티부문 당기순손실 원인 
- 급격한 환율 하락에 따른 약 800억원의 환산손실이 주요 원인

Q10. 대미 투자 관련 기자재 발주 시점 
- 미국 부지 확정, 웨스팅하우스 사업제안 및 한국 정부 심사·국회 승인 절차 필요 
- 연말~내년 초 발주 여부는 불확실하나 조기 발주 추진

Q11. 대미 투자 프로젝트의 기자재 공급 범위 확대 가능성 
- 원자로·증기발생기 외 추가 품목까지 공급 범위 확대를 위해 웨스팅하우스와 협상 중

Q12. 장주기 기자재 선발주 후 인허가 지연 시 실적 영향 
- 기자재 출하 시 수금 조건이 충족되며 선발주 자금도 사전 확보 
- 보글·VC 서머 사례에서 기자재 대금을 회수해 프로젝트 지연에 따른 실적 영향은 제한적

Q13. 4Q26 신재생·풍력 수주 목표 약 7,000억원의 달성 가능성 
- 해상풍력 기자재·EPC 계약 및 유지보수 계약 추진 중 
- 주요 계약 협의가 상당 부분 완료돼 일정 지연이 없다면 연내 목표 달성 전망

Q14. 중형 가스터빈 개발 및 해외 진출 계획 
- 대형 가스터빈은 한국·미국 중심으로 추진하며 중동 고객사와도 협의 중 
- 휴스턴 서비스 허브를 기반으로 미국 데이터센터 시장 대응 
- 중형 가스터빈은 시제품 제작 및 시험 진행 후 2028년 하반기 출하 목표

### Page 4

두산에너빌리티

4_ Eugene Research Center 
Q15. NuScale 모듈 1 기 선발주 배경 
- 대규모 일괄 발주에 대비한 제작 경험 확보 및 납기 리스크 완화를 위해 사전 제작 추진

Q16. X-energy 연내 후속 계약 대상 품목 
- 증기발생기 전열관 및 주단 소재 등 장주기 품목의 사전 발주 추진

Q17. DOE 프로젝트의 장주기 기자재 공급 범위 확대 가능성 
- 웨스팅하우스 공급망 상황에 따라 추가 품목 협상 중 
- 과거 보글·VC 서머보다 넓은 공급 범위 기대

Q18. 웨스팅하우스 지분 투자와 기자재 계약 시점의 연계 가능성 
- 지분 투자는 내년, 기자재 계약은 연내 또는 내년 초 목표로 시점이 일치할지는 불확실

Q19. X-energy 장주기 기자재의 소재·부품 구분 
- 증기발생기 전열관은 부품, 주단 소재는 소재에 해당 
- 전열관은 공급 병목에 대비해 조기 발주하고 주단 소재는 설계 확정 후 제작 준비

Q20. 복합화력 주기기 수주의 기존·신규 고객 비중 
- 기존 고객과 추가 예약계약을 협의 중이며 신규 글로벌 고객과도 예약계약 협상 진행

Q21. 주요 SMR 사업 외 파트너십 확대 가능성 
- Rolls-Royce 사업 추진 및 GE Hitachi BWRX-300 주단 소재 예약계약 체결 
- i-SMR은 2035년 운전을 목표로 2028년 이전 발주가 필요해 관련 협의 진행

Q22. X-energy 전열관·주단 소재 수주 물량 규모 
- 다우케미컬 초도 사업은 80MW 원자로 4 개 모듈로 구성되며, 해당 4 개 모듈분 부품·소재 공급

### Page 5

두산에너빌리티

Eugene Research Center _5 
Q23. 중동 지정학적 불안정에 따른 프로젝트·물류 영향 
- 기존 프로젝트의 운송비 증가분을 발주처·개발사와 협의 중 
- 신규 계약에는 비용 보상 및 공기 지연 관련 조항 반영

Q24. 가스터빈 생산능력 증설 규모 및 시기 
- 현재 생산능력 증설 중이며 2031년 출하분까지 예약 접수 
- 시장 수요에 따라 추가 증설 검토

Q25. 2030년 가스터빈 예약 물량의 실제 계약 전환 시점 
- 2030년 출하 물량은 대부분 내년 실제 계약 여부 결정 전망 
- 2031년 출하 물량은 2028년경 계약 여부 결정 예상

Compliance Notice 
당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에 동 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

투자기간 및 투자등급/투자의견 비율

Document 254: [373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적보상금 제외해도 호실적 - 기업

Source PDF: all report pdf/[373220] LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적LG에너지솔루션(373220) 보상금 제외해도 호실적보상금 제외해도 호실적 - 기업.pdf
Report source: Hankyung
Date: 2026-10-12
Pages: 10
Status: ok

### Page 1

(373220) 
LG 에너지솔루션 
2026.10.12

에너지/인프라/배터리 황성현, Ph.D. 
6878 / tjdgus2009@eugenefn.com

보상금 제외해도 호실적 
 투자의견 BUY(유지) 
 목표주가 530,000원(상향) 
 현재주가 401,000원(10/8)

 [투자의견] 당사는 LG에너지솔루션에 대해 실적 추정치 상향을 반영하여 목
표주가를 530,000원으로 13% 상향하고, 투자의견 BUY를 유지한다. 
3Q26부터 ESS 물량 증가에 따른 가동률 상승 효과가 본격적으로 실적에 
반영되기 시작했으며, 4Q26에도 AMPC 제외 흑자 기조를 이어갈 전망이
다. 전사 가동률은 3분기 65%까지 상승한 것으로 파악되며, 이에 따른 펀
더멘털 개선이 본격화되고 있다고 판단한다. 
 [3Q26 잠정 실적 Review] 매출액 9.6조원(+28%qoq, +59%yoy), 영업이
익 7,560억원(+567%qoq, +26%yoy)을 기록하며 당사 추정을 큰 폭으로 
상회했다. 북미 생산보조금(AMPC)을 제외한 매출액은 9.2조원, 영업이익은 
3,391억원(영업이익률 3.7%)으로, 2 분기(-1,277억원, -1.7%) 대비 흑자 
전환에 성공했다. ① 원통형은 테슬라향 물량 호조에 힘입어 영업이익 
2,010억원(영업이익률 8.1%)을 달성한 것으로 추정된다. ② EV는 유럽 폴
란드 공장의 가동률 상승과 GM 얼티엄의 8월 가동 개시로 매출이 증가했
다. 여기에 완성차 업체 보상금 3,000억원이 반영되며 영업이익 1,090억
원(영업이익률 2.5%)을 기록한 것으로 추정된다. ③ ESS는 매출액이 50% 
이상 급증하며 실적 서프라이즈를 견인했다. AMPC 포함 영업이익은 4,460
억원(영업이익률 16.0%)으로 추정된다. 2 분기 매출 성장률 +17%qoq에 
이어 3분기 성장세가 더욱 가팔라졌다는 점이 긍정적이다. 
 [4Q26 Preview] 매출액 8.0조원(-17%qoq, +30%yoy), 영업이익 6,790
억원(-10%qoq, 흑전yoy)으로 추정한다. 4 분기 실적은 ESS 수익성 개선 
속도와 완성차 업체 보상금 반영 규모에 따라 변동할 전망이다. 동사는 기존 
가이던스를 통해 하반기 북미 ESS 생산량이 상반기 대비 2배 증가할 것으
로 제시한 바 있다. 3분기 ESS는 AMPC를 제외하더라도 양호한 수익성을 
기록한 것으로 추정된다. 향후 팩 생산 병목 해소와 북미 공장 가동 안정화
에 따른 고정비 부담 완화가 이어지며 추가적인 손익 개선이 가능할 전망이
다. EV 역시 GM 얼티엄 생산 확대와 유럽 공장 가동률 상승이 지속되며 
AMPC 제외 적자 규모를 점진적으로 축소할 것으로 예상한다. 
 [투자포인트] 9월 미시간 랜싱 공장의 개소식이 개최됐다. 랜싱 공장은 당초 
EV용 파우치 배터리 생산거점으로 계획됐으나 ESS 중심으로 역할이 재편
됐으며, 내년에는 10GWh 이상의 각형 LFP 생산라인이 추가될 예정이다. 
2027년에는 신규 대규모 증설보다 기존 북미 생산거점의 가동률 상승과 
ESS 생산량 확대를 통한 수익성 개선이 핵심이 될 전망이다. 북미 전력망 
접속 지연과 AI 데이터센터의 부하 변동성 확대에 따라 Stand-alone ESS, 
BTM, UPS, BBU 수요가 증가하면서 신규 수주도 지속 확대될 것으로 예상
한다. 특히 동사의 Non-PFE 요건 충족과 SI· O&M을 아우르는 End-to-
End 솔루션 역량이 북미 ESS 시장에서 경쟁력으로 작용할 전망이다.

당사는 자료 작성일 기준으로 지난 3 개월 간 해당종목에 대해서 유가증권 발행에 참여한 적이 없습니다 
당사는 본 자료 발간일을 기준으로 해당종목의 주식을 1% 이상 보유하고 있지 않습니다  
당사는 동 자료를 기관투자가 또는 제 3 자에게 사전 제공한 사실이 없습니다 
조사분석담당자는 자료작성일 현재 동 종목과 관련하여 재산적 이해관계가 없습니다 
동 자료에 게재된 내용들은 조사분석담당자 본인의 의견을 정확하게 반영하고 있으며, 외부의 부당한 압력이나 간섭 없이 작성되었음을 확인합니다 
동 자료는 당사의 제작물로서 모든 저작권은 당사에게 있습니다 
동 자료는 당사의 동의 없이 어떠한 경우에도 어떠한 형태로든 복제, 배포, 전송, 변형, 대여할 수 없습니다 
동 자료에 수록된 내용은 당사 리서치센터가 신뢰할 만한 자료 및 정보로부터 얻어진 것이나, 당사는 그 정확성이나 완전성을 보장할 수 없습니다. 따라서 어떠
한 경우에 동 자료는 고객의 주식투자의 결과에 대한 법적 책임소재에 대한 증빙자료로 사용될 수 없습니다

Document 258: Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Advanced economies _ Resilience to shock after shock (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
History tends to repeat itself in advanced economies. Once again, growth ultimately fell short of expectations by only a small
margin in the first half of 2026, despite the conflict in Iran. As early as 2025, the impact of tariffs was less severe than feared.
This is a sign that structural factors (notably AI, defence and electrification) are underpinning growth in these countries

and are expected to continue to do so in 2027. However, there are significant risks to the economic outlook, primarily of a
geopolitical nature, with a significant upside risk to inflation and interest rates. The other instance of history repeating itself
concerns a period that those under 20 cannot have experienced: the period leading up to the 2008 crisis, echoes of which can
be seen in terms of growth, inflation, the direction of monetary policy, and long-term interest rates.

Advanced economies are experiencing trends similar to those they
faced nearly 20 years ago:
- Growth remains resilient in 2025-26 despite shocks, as it did in 2006-
07, in the major advanced economies.
- Inflation is above central banks’ targets: as in 2006-07, this calls for a
tightening of monetary policy.
- In Europe and the United States, long-term interest rates have re -
turned to their 2006-07 levels (1996 in Japan): a challenging environ-

ment for public finances in a context where public debt ratios are signi-
ficantly higher than those seen 20 years ago.
THE GLOBAL ECONOMY IN THE MIDST OF A GROWTH CYCLE
We are already well into 2026, and the picture so far is clear: glo -
bal growth is proving resilient. Following a US tariff shock last year,
growth is once again proving, for the time being, to be significantly less
affected by the conflict in Iran than had been anticipated when it first

broke out. Initially, however, there were fears that this new shock would
hit growth even harder. As with the tariff hike in 2025, the impact was
measurable, particularly in terms of consumer confidence. But in both
2025 and 2026, growth has also shown a degree of resilience.
The figures for the second quarter bear this out, despite the sharp rise
in oil prices, the recessionary impact of which is not yet apparent.
According to our forecasts, growth was expected to reach or exceed 1%

in 2026 in the four major advanced economies (the Eurozone, Japan,
the United Kingdom and the United States, where it exceeds 2%) for
the second consecutive year. This had not happened (excluding the
post-COVID catch-up period) since 2006-07. Growth figures are more
moderate (particularly for demographic reasons), but the private sec -
tor is not (at present) showing the same excesses (at that time, the
credit boom had preceded the worst crisis since the 1930s); although

debt levels have recently risen in the US tech sector.
The driving force behind the current growth cycle is clear: artificial
intelligence (AI). It is fuelling global trade and business investment
in most countries. This is clearly the case in the United States, where
business investment is growing strongly as investment plans in the
US tech sector are implemented.
However, the world’s major economies are currently engaged in a
broader push to expand their capital goods investment. Alongside the

ongoing development of services—particularly digital services—we
have seen a rebound in industrial production in most countries since
early 2025. The leading sectors are primarily technology equipment,
machinery, aeronautics, defence and their essential inputs (energy and
metals). This trend is evident everywhere, including in Europe, and
is becoming entrenched over the long term, with an improvement in
business climate indicators, particularly new export orders. Growth is

therefore expected to strengthen further in 2027, particularly in the
Eurozone, where German investment plans are set to gather momen -
tum and whose spillover effects will have a greater impact on the eco-
nomy.
However, not all sectors are experiencing the same momentum. This
is particularly true for the sectors that depend on household consump-
tion, which has shown mixed trends. Our observation that consumer
spending is more buoyant in the United States than in Europe was

confirmed in 2026, and the outlook does not point to a change in this
ranking.
EDITORIAL
RESILIENCE TO SHOCK AFTER SHOCK

### Page 2

EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
While European and US households are experiencing more or less the
same energy price shock, inflation remains higher in the United States.
But this has not prevented consumer spending from keeping pace, pri-
marily because it is driven by the wealthiest households, who are less
sensitive to inflation than to the wealth effect. Furthermore, while the

savings rate remains high in the Eurozone, US households have been
drawing down their savings. This is also true of British households,
which explains why the UK has seen slightly stronger growth since
the beginning of the year. In Japan, government spending has partially
offset energy inflation (while other countries are doing so to a much
lesser extent than in 2022), which has supported consumption growth.
However, these buffers—reduced savings or fiscal support—have their

limits when shocks persist. The two major current conflicts (Iran and
Ukraine) are far from being resolved, and their impact is continuing.
This persistent geopolitical risk and its implications for inflation repre-
sent a significant headwind to the growth outlook for 2027.
HIGHER INFLATION CALLS FOR MONETARY TIGHTENING
Inflation is exceeding the target set by three of the four central banks
examined in this publication, which is another similarity (albeit with

different underlying causes) with the 2006-07 period. Japan is the ex -
ception, as government support measures for households are tempo -
rarily holding back inflation.
Apart from the Bank of Japan, which is engaged in a long-term norma-
lisation process, no central bank was expected to raise its key rates
in 2026. But they are now being prompted to do so for reasons that
vary from one region to another. The first is the conflict in Iran and
the resulting rise in oil prices, which triggered a rebound in energy

inflation from the second quarter onwards. However, since the conflict
was not resolved by the memorandum of understanding signed in June,
and given that the subsequent drop in oil prices proved short-lived,
inflation is expected to rise again. The rise in gas prices is expected to
exacerbate the impact, particularly in Europe, where inventories are
being replenished ahead of winter. Electricity prices are expected to
follow suit in most countries, but at a more moderate pace than in

2022. Indeed, gas is now used less to generate electricity than it was
at that time (thanks to the expansion of renewable energy), and to an
even lesser extent in France.
There is a significant risk that energy inflation will spread further to
other components of inflation in the future, and inflation expectations
could eventually reflect this. This is why the European Central Bank
has already raised its key interest rate twice (in June and September)

and, according to our scenario, will likely do so again in December. For
the same reason, we expect the Bank of England to raise its policy rate
in November. Although the cap on energy prices was maintained for
a long time, it has now been raised. As a result, inflation is expected
to rise between now and the end of the year. The aim will then be to
prevent second-round effects.
Inflation can also be a sign of (too much) economic strength. In the

United States, it has exceeded the 2% target since 2021. The main
argument in favour of a new cycle of rate hikes (following the one in
2022-2023) lies in the current resilience of growth and the improving
labour market. The Federal Reserve (Fed) must balance two equally
important objectives: maximum employment and price stability. Howe-
ver, unlike in the 4 th quarter of 2025, when the decline in job crea -
tion justified monetary easing (by 75 basis points), the current sharp

rebound in job creation completely reverses that analysis. According
to our forecasts, the unemployment rate is expected to decrease in
the coming months, which pushes back the prospect of a decline in
core inflation. This situation persists due to both supply constraints
(over which a central bank has no control) and excess demand (which
the Fed can, however, mitigate). Consequently, the Fed raised its key
rate in September and is expected to implement two further rate hikes

between now and January 2027.
In Japan, finally, while headline inflation has been brought down by
government support measures, core inflation remains significant.
Wage pressures are mounting against a backdrop of labour shortages.
The Bank of Japan is therefore being forced to accelerate the pace of its
monetary normalisation.
In all these regions, inflation is currently expected to peak in Q4 2026
or Q1 2027. It is against this backdrop of temporary inflation that rate

hikes could also come to an end by that time (excluding Japan). Howe-
ver, this assumes that once the energy shock—the course of which
remains uncertain—subsides, inflation will fall. The effects of El Niño,
which are still difficult to gauge at present, could take over. Inflation
would then remain above the central banks’ target for a longer period.
Under this scenario, rate hikes would go further than is currently en -
visaged.
THE RISE IN LONG- TERM INTEREST RATES: A FACTOR THAT WILL WEIGH

ON PUBLIC FINANCES
Monetary policies have also to offset the inflationary effects of fiscal
policy, particularly in the United States (where the budget deficit is
high and shows no signs of shrinking) and in Japan (where the new
government is pursuing pro-growth policies). In Europe, fiscal policy
is playing a much smaller role than it did in 2022. While the stimulus
measures implemented at that time did help reduce the inflation rate
in the short term (by nearly 2.5 pp in France, for example), by keeping

demand at a high level, they delayed disinflation and increased the
scale of the monetary tightening required.
The outbreak of the conflict in Iran acted as a catalyst: it triggered a
rise in long-term rates (which had been trending downwards at the
start of the year) to levels close to those of 2006-07. This rise is wides-
pread, but its causes vary. Inflation expectations appear to play a more
significant role in the Eurozone, whereas the rise in real interest rates

seems to be the main factor in the United States and Japan.

The bank
for a changing
world
Higher inflation and more resilient real growth than feared point in
the same direction: stronger than expected nominal growth that is li -
kely to be sustained. However, in the long term, high nominal growth
tends to drive up nominal interest rates (as it is a positive sign of more
profitable investment projects), in contrast to the problem of secular

stagnation raised in the 2010s (which combined low nominal growth
with low nominal interest rates).
The other issue is the high level of fiscal deficits (too high for a period
of growth), with various situations. In several countries, the public
debt-to-GDP ratio continues to rise. In the United States, deficit reduc-
tion does not appear to be a priority, to the extent that it is expected
to remain above 6% of GDP at the very least until the end of Donald

Trump’s term of office (with little hope that the mid-term elections will
significantly alter this trend). In the United Kingdom and France, fiscal
consolidation has begun, but it is being hampered by the simultaneous
increase in certain areas of expenditure (debt servicing, defence and
healthcare, due in particular to an ageing population), which is slowing
the pace of consolidation. In Germany, the implementation of invest -
ment plans is leading to a widening of the deficit. While this does not

pose a sustainability problem, it does result in an increase in annual
debt issuance. In Japan, the budget deficit is kept in check by interest
payments that remain low, as the rise in interest rates is recent and
the average maturity of the debt is high (10 years). However, expansio-
nary fiscal policy could pose further problems in terms of debt dyna -
mics if it were to continue through the end of the decade. In Italy and
Spain, fiscal consolidation has been sufficient to stabilise the public

debt-to-GDP ratio (and even to reduce it in Spain), but the high level of
Italy’s debt service it vulnerable to a rise in interest rates.
In all these countries, the fiscal arithmetic is simpler today than it
will be in 2030. Indeed, in most cases, only half of the debt was is -
sued after 2022 (that is, after the end of the zero-interest-rate period).
The debt service has so far only partially risen, except in the United
States, where most of the increase has already occurred due to a shor-

ter average debt maturity (6 years). This means that, for all countries
combined, the rate of GDP growth remains higher than the average
interest rate paid on public debt (or the effective interest rate).
By 2030, the effective rate will have risen and caught up with
GDP growth. As a result, in most cases, the debt service will have re -
turned to the level as a share of GDP seen in the early 2000s. In order to
stabilise public debt-to-GDP ratios, primary balances will need to have

improved. This will even require the generation of primary surpluses,
particularly in Italy (which is already achieving this), France and the
United Kingdom. This is a crucial shift that governments will need to
introduce as early as the 2027 budget; otherwise, they risk postponing
the stabilisation of their public debt-to-GDP ratio once again.
Article completed on 17 September 2026

### Page 1

以上資料為元大證券國際金融業務部依上手提供研究報告摘譯，僅供內部教育訓練使用。 
Anthropic 招股書裡最值得看的 14 個細節 
2026/10/27

摘要： 
⚫ 華爾街見聞整理了人工智慧獨角獸 Anthropic 提交 S-1 招股書的核心數據與關鍵細節。公司
擬以代碼「ANT」登陸那斯達克，目標估值介於 1.5 兆至 2 兆美元之間。 
⚫ 營收五個季度漲了約 30 倍，第二季已經轉盈，上半年帳面卻虧了 807 億美元，算力帳單還簽
到了 2045 年……Anthropic 招股書裡最值得看的 14 個細節，不少都帶著這樣的反差。更少見
的是控制權：普通股東幾乎沒有話語權，50.1%的投票權握在七位創辦人手中。 
1. 瘋狂的營收增速與放緩跡象  
⚫ 營收從 2023 年的 4,100 萬美元飆升至 2025 年的 45.85 億美元，2026 上半年達 
162.86 億美元；第二季單季營收 115.55 億美元，五個季度內暴增約 30 倍。今年前 9 
個月累計收入突破 330 億美元，過去一年人均創收達 660 萬美元。不過第二季至第三季
營收季增幅已放緩至接近 50%，公司警示早期爆發增長後未來可能停滯。

### Page 2

以上資料為元大證券國際金融業務部依上手提供研究報告摘譯，僅供內部教育訓練使用。 
2. 半年帳面虧損 807 億美元，主因為公允價值變動  
⚫ 2026 上半年帳面淨虧損達 807.48 億美元，但其中約 723.88 億美元是因公司估值大幅
上升，導致可轉換票據、衍生負債與 SAFE 評估價值提高所認列的帳面虧損，並無現金流
出。扣除股權激勵等非現金費用後，上半年調整後營業利潤為 16.18 億美元，調整後淨利
潤達 26.71 億美元。 
3. 算力成本佔比驟降，經營利潤成功轉正  
⚫ 每 1 美元營收對應的算力與基礎設施支出，從 2025 年第一季的超過 2.5 美元降至 
2026 年第二季的不足 50 美分（算力支出佔比由 2023 年的 10 倍以上降至 2026 上
半年的 55%）。2026 年第二季經營利潤達 9.74 億美元（利潤率 8%），上半年營運現
金流淨流入 39 億美元，主要受惠於「閉環研發」模型訓練效率提升、推理優化、跨晶片
自建調度及降價策略。

4. 客戶結構多元化，頭部集中度下降  
⚫ 今年前 9 個月前五大客戶貢獻 25% 營收，但佔比從第一季的約 36% 降至第三季的 
21%，已無單一客戶佔比超過 10%。年消費破 10 萬美元的客戶超過 11,900 家，超過
半數名列財富 500 強（但其支出佔自身總預算不到 0.1%）。淨收入留存率（不含訂閱）
高達約 900%，且第三季有超過 80% 的營收來自近 6 個月內發布的新模型。

### Page 3

以上資料為元大證券國際金融業務部依上手提供研究報告摘譯，僅供內部教育訓練使用。

5. 5%至 7%的收入來自違規客戶  
⚫ 前 9 個月約有 5% 至 7%（約 16 億至 23 億美元）的營收來自事後被判定違反使用規
範或商業條款的客戶，主因是監控、調查至封號存在時間差。隨著 8、9 月加大封禁力
度，此類收入佔比將下降，可能對未來的營收與增速造成一定拉低。 
6. 算力長約承諾超 8,000 億美元，最遠鎖定至 2045 年  
⚫ 各項固定或保底採購、已簽約未起算之租約及後續長期合約合計超過 8,300 億美元，包括
博通（五年 1,610 億美元租約及後續最高 1,500 億美元支出）、AMD（逾 200 億美
元）、三大雲端巨頭（十年至少 2,694 億美元）等。合約多採能源長約常見的「照付不
議」（用量不足仍須補足差額）條款，成本提前鎖定，而公司 80% 收入卻採依用量實際
結算，形成節奏差異。

### Page 4

以上資料為元大證券國際金融業務部依上手提供研究報告摘譯，僅供內部教育訓練使用。
7. 每月支付給馬斯克的 SpaceX 算力費高達 31 億美元
⚫ Anthropic 租用 SpaceX.AI（原 xAI）位於孟菲斯的資料中心，合約總額最高達 1,205
億美元，合約至 2029 年。12 月算力全面上線後每月費用約 31 億美元，該月費已接近
SpaceX 今年首季整個 AI 業務營收（8.18 億美元）的四倍之多。
8. 巨頭持股版圖與極短的解禁安排
⚫ 若以 2 兆美元估值計算，亞馬遜全面轉股後約持股近 20%（約 4,000 億美元，超出
12% 上限部分暫以票據保留），Google 持股約 7% 並擁有後續增資與基石投資權限。
解禁期不同於傳統 180 天限制，上市後 76 天內設五個解禁日，首日即允許非創辦人董
事、高管與部分員工減持套現。
9. 特殊雙層治理架構，創辦團隊握有 50.1%絕對投票權
⚫ 普通股東對決策幾乎無控制權，亞馬遜與 Google 持有的 N 類股亦無投票權。公司頂層

由「長期利益信託」（LTBT）掌控制衡，而七位共同創辦人則透過僅 1 股、每人出資 1
美元認購的「F 類股」掌握 50.1% 的絕對投票權，屬於那斯達克規則下的「受控公
司」。
10. 股價每漲 50%，最高提撥 2%股權投入公益
⚫ 上市前已將全面攤薄股本的 5%（約 8.47 億股 N 類股）劃入 Anthropic 公益基金。董
事會批准未來股價若從發行價起每上漲 50%，可再額外注入最高 2% 股份；同時七位共
同創辦人承諾將個人 80% 的股權收益用於公益捐贈。
11. 長達 90 多頁的「生存級」風險因子揭露
⚫ 篇幅約為 SpaceX 的三倍，直接提及技術可能對人類構成「災難性或生存級風險」、可能
被惡意行為者用於開發致命武器，甚至可能衍生出模型抵制關機、操縱資訊或勒索等失控
行為。招股書直言，此類系統性風險恐怕連整個全球保險業都無力承保。
12. 創辦團隊向投資者發出的理念警告
⚫ 七位創辦人公開呼籲投資人切勿純以短期財務交易視角看待公司，直言部分商業決策會在
公共利益與利潤最大化之間權衡，作法可能與股東預期背道而馳。同時，團隊也著重強調

創始成員至今未流失，與競爭對手 OpenAI 形成對比。
13. 上市股票代碼為「ANT」，展現低自我企業文化
⚫ Anthropic 將以股票代碼「ANT」在那斯達克掛牌。招股書以「low-ego」（低自我）形
容內部文化，員工內部自稱為「Ants」（螞蟻），形成文化與代碼的巧妙呼應。

14. 市場下一步檢驗指標：10 月 ARR 表現  
⚫ 市場高度關注 S-1 補充文件中將揭露的 10 月 Run-Rate Revenue（以近 28 天用量年
化加計訂閱費）。目前市場預期 730 億美元為合理基準，若突破 780 億美元，將大幅提

IPO 估值的關鍵密碼。

Anthropic 计划在美国 IPO 中⾯向⽇本投资者募集最多 35 亿美元，瑞穗证券将主导⽇本部分

摘要：
Anthropic PBC 计划在其美国 IPO 中⾯向⽇本投资者最多募集 35 亿美元，将这⼀⾥程碑式的股
票发⾏引⼊⽇本 —— ⼀个新股供应已降⾄ 14 年低点的市场。
瑞穗证券将负责管理此次发⾏的⽇本部分，⾼盛、三菱 UFJ 摩根⼠丹利证券及 Morgan Stanley
MUFG Securities 等机构将参与处理投资者订单，瑞穗⾦融集团与三菱 UFJ ⾦融集团亦列于承销

商之列。这⼀安排意味着⽇本成为少数可直接参与此次⼤型 IPO 的市场之⼀，与之并列的还有澳
⼤利亚、加拿⼤以及欧洲部分地区的投资者。
在 AI 领域巨型融资接连登场的背景下， Anthropic 此次 IPO 将成为市场胃⼝的⼀次重要检验。
Meta Platforms 正寻求通过出售普通股及优先股筹资最多 950 亿美元， Alphabet 则寻求最多 850
亿美元的股权融资，多只 AI 相关股票的密集发⾏将考验投资者的承接能⼒。
IPO 时机：企业竞相叩开股票市场⼤⻔
⽇本市场的特殊供需背景，使其成为此次发⾏的重要⽬标地。根据监管⽂件，⽇本新股发⾏已
萎缩⾄ 14 年来的低位，投资者⾯临优质新股稀缺的困境，这为 Anthropic 提供了相对有利的发⾏
窗⼝。
Anthropic 上⽉提交了这份期待已久的 IPO 申请，加⼊了近期企业密集寻求股权融资的浪潮。公
司旗下拥有 Claude 聊天机器⼈，是当前最受关注的 AI 基础模型公司之⼀。
此次发⾏的规模与时机均具标志性意义。在 Meta Platforms 和 Alphabet 相继谋求数百亿美元量级

股权融资的同期， Anthropic 的 IPO 将与之共同检验市场对 AI 相关股票新⼀轮密集供给的承受能
⼒，以及机构投资者在当前环境下的⻛险偏好。

2 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Consumers Feel One Way, but Spend Another
Kirsten Cabacungan, Vice President and Senior Investment Strategist
Americans have spent much of the past several years telling surveys they are cautious
about the economy. Yet they continue to spend as though they are not. The University
of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index remains near multidecade lows, while the
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence has also been in a yearslong downtrend.

These surveys reflect a list of persistent concerns from elevated food and gas prices to
geopolitical uncertainty and labor market anxiety.
Even so, consumer spending has remained remarkably resilient. The final estimate of Q2
real consumer spending, as measured by PCE, was revised up to an annualized rate of
3.8%, well above the 2.3% 20-year average. More recent data suggest that momentum
has continued. According to Bank of America Institute, the latest monthly total credit and

debit card spending data per household rose 6.2% year-over-year (YoY) in September, and
spending excluding gasoline was up 5.1% YoY, both the third strongest growth rates in the
past four years. 1
The persistence of consumer spending is particularly notable given that temporary cyclical
boosts like larger tax refunds are starting to fade. That raises an important question: What is
actually funding strong consumption today? Recent data suggest the drivers are becoming

increasingly more diversified, with broadening labor market trends, healthy consumer
finances and elevated wealth effects all reinforcing one another and underpinning consumers.
First, the labor market continues to provide an important foundation for consumer spending,
and recent trends suggest wage growth is supporting a broader set of households than in
recent years. Jobs growth has slowed from the exceptionally strong pace seen following the
pandemic, but broader indicators suggest labor market conditions remain relatively stable.

Although the September jobs report came in softer than expected, the three- and six-month
rolling averages indicate that the trend in jobs growth likely bottomed out last year and has
gradually improved since (Exhibit 1A). Meanwhile, the unemployment rate remains low by
historical standards and layoffs remain contained. And while wage growth has moderated
and recently trailed headline inflation (Exhibit 1B), cumulative income gains over the past
several years have likely helped many households absorb higher prices better.

An important development though has been improving wage growth trends among lower-
income households, where spending tends to be most sensitive to changes in wages. Bank
of America Institute consumer deposit account data show after-tax wage growth for lower-
income households outpaced higher-income households for a second consecutive month
in August, rising 4.7% YoY compared with 3.5% for higher-income households. Importantly,
this improvement appears to be supporting discretionary spending, not just spending

on necessities. Lower-income discretionary spending rose 5.7% YoY in August, nearly
matching the 5.9% pace for higher-income households. In other words, the narrowing gap
in wage and spending growth across income cohorts suggests consumer resilience looks
to be becoming less dependent on affluent households alone, creating a broader and more
durable foundation for consumption.
Second, household finances remain in relatively good shape, providing consumers with the

flexibility to continue spending. The ratio of household debt to disposable personal income
remains near its lowest level since the late 1990s, excluding the temporary distortions
caused by pandemic-related income support in 2020 and 2021 (Exhibit 1C). Recent
revisions to the Bureau of Economic Analysis national income accounts also suggest
consumers have a larger financial buffer than previous data suggested. Personal saving
estimates over the past year have been revised higher by $320 billion, or 39%, and the

revised savings rate remains above 4% after previous estimates showed it fell to 3% during
the summer.2 Concerns that consumers may be rapidly exhausting their savings buffers
may therefore be overstated. Supporting this view, Bank of America Institute consumer
1 Bank of America Institute, “Consumer Checkpoint: Strength in numbers.” As of October 9, 2026.
2 Bank of America, “Massive Savings Markup = Another Reason to Be Bullish.” As of October 2, 2026.

Investment Implications
Consumer spending strength
remains a key pillar of economic
and earnings growth, which keeps
us constructive on our Equity
overweight. The growing connection
between household wealth and
spending reinforces the importance
of maintaining a diversified portfolio.

### Page 3

3 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
deposit account data show that middle- and lower-income households continue to maintain
deposit balances above prepandemic levels across age groups.
Third, elevated household wealth continues to provide a powerful tailwind for consumer
spending, particularly among households that account for an outsized share of overall
consumption. U.S. household net worth rose $12.8 trillion (T) to a record $195.9T in Q2,

driven largely by gains in Equities, which contributed $10.7T of the increase (Exhibit 1D).
As a result, household net worth relative to disposable income climbed to a record 8.3,
meaning households now hold more than eight dollars of wealth for every dollar of annual
disposable income. Households are also sitting on $35.8T in home equity, an all-time high
level that provides a significant balance sheet cushion. Rising asset values have helped to

support spending through what economists refer to as wealth effects. In other words, when
households feel wealthier, they tend to be more willing to spend.
For investors, the key takeaway is that consumer spending is being supported by multiple
engines despite the weaker sentiment data. Labor income trends are supporting wider
participation, healthy balance sheets are helping households absorb economic pressures,
and elevated household wealth continues to sustain higher-income spending. Together,

these forces are supportive of economic growth and corporate profits, which is particularly
important since the consumer accounts for roughly 70% of U.S. gross domestic product.
That said, it does not eliminate risk. A meaningful risk to the consumer would be a scenario
in which both the labor and wealth engines weaken simultaneously. For now, labor market
conditions remain stable, and wealth effects remain supportive. Equity market pullbacks in
recent years have done little to derail consumer spending, suggesting it would likely take a

deeper and more prolonged drawdown to alter consumer behavior.

MARKET VIEW
The Bull Market Turns Four: Older, Unloved—But Still Kicking
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Ariana Chiu, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Four years ago this month, virtually no one was in the mood to buy U.S. Equities, and for
good reasons.
Rewind to October 2022: Inflation the month before clocked in at an annual rate of 8.2%.

The yield curve was inverted, a signal that has preceded most past recessions. Consumer
sentiment was abysmal, having dropped to a record low in June 2022. Recession
concerns were mounting, with an October Wall Street Journal poll survey putting the
odds of a recession within 12 months at about 63%. And realizing (belatedly) that
inflation was not “transitory,” the Fed was in the midst of administering one of the most
aggressive monetary tightening cycles in decades, raising the fed funds rate five times

between March and September 2022 by a stunning 300 basis points.
Reflecting the mood at the moment, the S&P 500 closed at 3,577 on October 12, 2022,
down 25% below its January peak, leaving investors battered and bruised but blind to the
fact that the market had bottomed. Four years on, the bull market that emerged from
the gloom of 2022 is still standing and kicking, with the S&P 500 up roughly 130% since
the October 2022 lows.
The more than doubling of the S&P over the past four years has hardly been linear—and

has been accompanied with plenty of pain and doubt. Exhibit 2 maps the rocky path
trodden by the bull over the past four years. Less than six months into the rally, the
markets were rocked by the failure of Silicon Valley Bank (SVB) on March 10, 2023. The
S&P Regional Banks Select Industry Index lost 18% that week and stoked concerns of
massive deposit runs and credit stress across the capital markets. However, owing to
emergency intervention by the government, lower yields and strength elsewhere in the

markets, the crisis passed. Some banks failed that month, but the bull market didn’t.
Exhibit 2: Revisiting the Rocky Path Trodden by the Bull.
Period "Reason to Sell" What happened instead
October 2022 Inflation >8%, aggressive Fed tightening, recession concerns Bull market begins
March 2023 SVB/regional banking crisis Stocks keep climbing
2023-24 Inverted yield curve, “recession inevitable” Economy keeps expanding
2024 “Magnificent 7* bubble,” narrow breadth, expensive valuations Earnings propel market higher

2025 Tariffs, trade war, policy uncertainty Market absorbs another shock
2026 Oil/diesel shock, 5%+ yields, Fed tightening, geopolitical risks Bull reaches fourth birthday
*Apple, Amazon, Alphabet, Nvidia, Meta, Microsoft and Tesla. Source: Chief Investment Office. As of October 9, 2026.
But recessionary concerns hung like threatening clouds over the markets for most of
2023. Aggressive Fed tightening, an inverted yield curve, and soft economic data were

all taken as harbingers of an economic slowdown and profits recession. Yet the S&P 500
continued to climb in 2023, up 26.3% for the year, driven in large part by the outsized
price performance of the Magnificent Seven.
Investors spent much of 2024 fretting about a market too dependent on technology,
and therefore a market too narrow and too expensive. The unwinding of the Japan carry
trade in August 2024 engendered more angst, as did uncertainties around the 2024

U.S. presidential election. But despite a host of misgivings, the S&P 500 delivered total
returns of 25% in 2024. The unloved bull marched on.
Total returns in 2025 were robust as well—roughly 18%—notwithstanding—yet
again—a number of headwinds. In late January, DeepSeek, a cheaper China AI model,
raised questions about America’s technology leadership and the need to spend enormous
sums on chips and data centers. The Nasdaq promptly dropped by 3.1% thereafter.

Investment Implications
The adoption phase of the AI buildout
should translate to broader earnings
growth and performance across
sectors, reinforcing our conviction
in U.S. Equities. Given a strong
fundamental backdrop, we would
continue to buy on weakness as the
bull enters its fifth year

### Page 5

5 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
January’s jolt was followed by “Liberation Day,” or the warning of punishing U.S. tariffs
on America’s major trading partners. The trade-policy shock of April 2025 caused the
S&P 500 to drop nearly 19% from its February high to its April 8 low on uncertainty
that higher import costs would decrease corporate margins and consumer spending and
upend global supply chains. Amid the trade tumult, Treasury prices fell and the U.S. dollar

weakened on concerns that foreigners were abandoning U.S. assets. Remember all the
angst over the dollar debasement trade? If that wasn’t enough, expensive valuations and
enormous capital commitments raised doubts about the AI trade and triggered talk of an
AI bubble. Yet despite this wall of worry, the S&P 500, for the third year running, posted
double-digit gains in 2025.
And double-digit gains could be in store this year, with the S&P 500 up 14% through

October 9. The bull has rumbled on, taking in stride the energy shock from the war in
the Middle East, the shift in interest rate expectations from the Fed (from rate cuts
to rate hikes), and the rising uncertainty associated with the midterm elections. These
headwinds have been countered by tailwinds such as better-than-expected corporate
earnings and better-than-anticipated real growth.
Finally, as Exhibit 3 makes clear, what has given this bull so much muscle has been

the U.S. Technology sector, specifically the Magnificent 7. Powering the bull has been
the Magnificent 7, with a staggering 344% return since the 2022 lows, while U.S.
technology has delivered a 277% return versus the S&P 500’s return of 130%. Note the
outperformance of the S&P 500 relative to the main international indices.
Looking forward: Is the bull’s greatest strength also its Achilles’ heel? Bull
markets don’t die of natural causes. And they typically don’t expire because of one

particular signal or indicator. Bull markets turn to bear markets when a cluster of signals/
metrics turn south. Think deteriorating and downward earnings revisions, higher interest
rates, widening credit spreads and tighter lending standards, growth disappointments,
stretched valuations and sentiment, deteriorating market breadth, and an external
shock. Any one of these signals could flash red and not trouble the bull.  But when they
converge or feed off each other, that’s trouble.

In addition, since a great deal of the earnings growth, market leadership and capital
expenditures (capex) spending of this bull market have come from the technology sector,
the bull’s greatest strength could become its greatest vulnerability. AI has become an
investment ecosystem unto itself, and hence the fifth year of the bull market will pivot
in part around AI revenue, frontier model adoption rates, infrastructure spending, free
cash flows, depreciation costs, debt issuance, data center utilization, cloud growth, and

competition via cheaper models and computing alternatives. To put it mildly, there is a
great deal at stake when it comes to the bull and AI.
However, this is where we stand today: We believe the bull will continue to rumble
ahead next year, with AI revenues and productivity gains justifying the costs of the AI
infrastructure. Critically, AI is moving from the buildout phase, dominated by chipmakers
and hyperscaler capex, into the adoption phase, where software firms, healthcare,

finance, energy and a myriad of other firms start to see productivity gains and margin
expansion from rising AI adoption rates. We believe this will give better breadth to the
S&P 500; so too will continued profit growth across various U.S. sectors, AI investment
that starts to pay off, and an economy capable of absorbing setbacks or periodic bouts
of uncertainty.
The path forward won’t be linear—and there will be fits and starts, and market
drawdowns. That’s normal for any market. But that aside, the bull will keep rumbling,

roaming and kicking in 2027.

6 of 8 October 13, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THE TICKER TAPE
What is our view of the macro and market landscape heading
into 2027 and beyond?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
A supply side-led economic expansion continues, driven by capital investment in the AI
buildout, solid consumer spending and fiscal tailwinds still blowing, albeit with less force.
This creates a backdrop that keeps nominal growth well-above trend and, importantly,

above the interest cost on national debt.
The interest rate backdrop largely remains the same as of today, but, instead of the
sharp increases that we experienced in 2026, the curve should settle down, and yields
should remain relatively range bound. The Fed is likely to tap on the breaks a couple of
more times before reassessing new inflation, growth and employment trends later in
2027.
Concerns surrounding credit risk and "circular" financing are likely to rise the further we

move into the AI buildout, but we do not expect a default cycle to develop. Rather, we
expect the sharp backup in yields to place pressure namely on lower-quality credits in
areas having a difficult time building the cash flow necessary to pay the higher interest
costs.
We expect a broadening out within the market but technology to still maintain its
heavyweight positioning. As we move from training and evolving the large language
models to agentic, and physical AI new leaders are likely to develop. In addition, we

anticipate AI adopters such as biotechnology, transport, logistics, advance manufacturing
and financial companies to enter new growth and productivity phases.
Lastly, overall earnings growth in the U.S. should be low to mid double-digit percent
stages in 2027, which is about half of the growth from 2026 thus far but still around
double from normal levels.
Credit risks developing and higher energy prices for longer are the main risks to our bull
case for Equities extending further into 2027 and beyond.

Index Definitions
ecurities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

University of Michigan’s Consumer Sentiment Index is a monthly survey of consumer confidence levels in the United States conducted by the University of Michigan.
Conference Board’s Index of Consumer Confidence measures what consumers are feeling about their expected financial situation, whether that's optimistic or pessimistic.
Consumer Price Index is a statistical measure that tracks the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a representative "market basket" of everyday goods and

services.
Nasdaq Composite Index is a stock market measure that tracks the performance of more than 3,000 stocks listed on the Nasdaq exchange.
MSCI Emerging Markets c is a stock market index that tracks the performance of large-cap and mid-cap companies across 24 developing and fast-growing nations.
MSCI All-Country World Index ex USA is a market-capitalization-weighted stock market index that tracks large- and mid-cap equity performance across developed and emerging markets

worldwide, excluding the United States.
MSCI Europe, Australasia, and the Far East (EAFE) Index is a stock market index that measures the performance of large- and mid-cap companies across 21 developed.
Dow Jones Industrial Average Index is a stock market index that tracks 30 major public companies listed on the New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) and Nasdaq.
S&P 500 Equal-Weighted Index is an alternative version of the standard S&P 500 that gives every constituent company an identical weight of 0.2% during each quarterly rebalance, rather

than weighting companies by their market capitalization.
Russell 2000 Index is a stock market index that tracks the performance of approximately 2,000 small-cap (small-capitalization) public companies in the United States.

volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.

#cliffnotes #rba #australia #inflation #us #fomc #ecb #aud #usd
Cliff Notes: managing expectations
By Ryan Wells, Illiana Jain & Elliot Clarke
11:04 October 23 2026 Share
Key insights from the week that was.
With little domestic data of significance, participants’ attention focused on RBA Deputy Governor
Hauser's fireside chat, which saw an abrupt shift in market expectations of a follow-up rate hike at the
November meeting.
Hauser warned in his remarks that the current inflation outlook was "materially worse" than the RBA's

### Page 2

August forecasts. Three upside inflation risks were characterised as having "crystallised" ahead of the
September meeting – the Middle East, the AI boom, and domestic supply constraints. Hauser noted the
Middle East picture "looks worse" given oil prices are "about a third higher than [the RBA] assumed in
August”. He then positioned AI and data centre developments among an "extraordinary sequence of
positive demand shocks around the world", with "higher AI prices, tech prices feeding through into

CPI". Finally, on domestic capacity pressures, the Deputy Governor noted that "inflation's been coming
in at or a little bit above expectations, despite an apparent easing in the labour market". He went on to
state that the staff are currently assessing "whether [the RBA] need to change [its] judgement on
capacity pressures in the labour market again" – in other words, revise up their assumption for the
NAIRU.
Hauser's comments prompted a material reassessment of market pricing for a follow-up November

hike, from just a 20% chance at the end of last week to a 50/50 proposition today ahead of next week's
Q3 CPI. For that release, we are forecasting a 1.3% (4.1%yr) lift in headline inflation and a 0.9 (3.5%yr)
increase in trimmed mean inflation. Our preview provides more detail behind the component mix for Q3
as well as our upward revisions to the preliminary Q4 forecast. Provided inflation prints broadly in line
with our expectations, the RBA is likely to hike by 25bp at the November meeting.

Across in New Zealand, consumer prices rose 0.7% in Q3 and annual inflation eased from 4.1%yr to
3.7%yr. Under the surface of this ‘as expected’ read though, several closely watched measures of core
inflation took a step higher to now be at or slightly above the top of the RBNZ’s target band. Our NZ
economics team consequently expect the RBNZ will raise the OCR 25bps to 3.00% at its October
policy review, while retaining a 4.00% terminal OCR forecast for September 2027.

Earlier in the week, China's economy surprised to the upside in Q3, GDP growth accelerating from
0.9%qtr to 1.2%qtr, lifting annual growth from 4.3%yr to 4.5%yr – keeping authorities on track to
achieve their 4.5-5.0% full-year growth objective.
While annual retail sales growth improved in September, rising from 0.4%yr to 1.1%yr, the current pace
of growth is only a quarter of the gain seen just a year ago and very weak versus history. Consumer
spending continues to be constrained by weak confidence, particularly with respect to employment

prospects and wealth. On housing wealth, five years into the property downturn, house prices continue
to decline month-on-month and investment remains in a deep downturn, -20.6%ytd.
Total fixed asset investment has been detracting from annual GDP growth since April owing to a lull in
high-tech investment, a lack of confidence in future demand amongst domestically oriented businesses
as well as property’s malaise. For aggregate growth to hold up through 2027 and 2028 as the

contribution from net exports moderates, broad-based stimulus in scale will prove necessary. Expect
recent targeted support measures to be broadened and given much greater scale in coming months.
Finally, in the UK, inflation accelerated from 3.1%yr to 3.5%yr in September, exceeding both market
expectations and the Bank of England's July forecast. The surprise was largely driven by energy, fuel
prices rising 5.4%mth. However, core inflation also edged up from 2.6%yr to 2.8%yr as services

inflation increased from 3.4%yr to 3.7%yr. While not an immediate concern inflation, the labour market
showing resilience, the ILO unemployment rate falling from 4.9% to 4.7% in August, suggests demand

### Page 3

Related articles
risks for inflation will need close monitoring as well as supply-side concerns.
Next week will see the focus once again return to the US as the FOMC meet and US Q3 GDP is
released. For both events, the implications for risks to the outlook will be the focus. The week after, the
US mid-term elections are due.

Document 340: Germany _ An export-driven recovery (2026-09-24)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Germany _ An export-driven recovery (2026-09-24).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-09-24

### Page 1

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
AN EXPORT-DRIVEN RECOVERY
Growth, which began in the fourth quarter of 2025, gained momentum in the first half of 2026. This momentum should
continue, with expected growth of 1.1% in 2026 and 1.3% in 2027. In the short term, the main driver will still be exports,
particularly to Europe, which will contribute to restructuring industry. The impact of investment plans is expected to

strengthen in 2027, while domestic demand is likely to remain constrained by inflation (2.7% in 2026 and 2.6 % in 2027),
which will weigh on purchasing power. Fiscal stimulus and structural reforms are expected to continue to support growth.
However, the rise in the deficit and debt levels means that the increase in long-term interest rates observed so far is mostly
structural.
A RETURN TO GROWTH DRIVEN BY EXTERNAL TRADE
The first half of 2026 confirms the rebound in growth (0.4% and then

0.3% q/q in Q1 and Q2). This growth is driven by foreign trade (contribu-
tions from net exports of +0.8 pp and +0.2 pp, respectively, with +0.9 pp
on average coming from exports and -0.4 pp from imports), following
two years of contracting exports, particularly those to Europe (see be -
low). The increase in public expenditure ( see our analysis ) reflects
the implementation of investment plans, with the multiplier effect on
growth of these plans expected to increase.

In the second half of the year, growth is expected to remain robust,
pushing the annual average to 1.1% in 2026 (following 0.3% in 2025).
Exports are set to remain the main driver, thanks to continued strong
European demand, which is compensating for domestic demand that
remains sluggish. Growth of private consumption will, in fact, remain
subdued by energy inflation. Business investment is expected to be
driven by dynamic sectors (defence, technology equipment and aeros -

pace), which would limit the impact of rising interest rates.
In 2027, growth (1.3%) would be based more heavily on domestic de -
mand, with greater spillover effects from investment plans and a likely
recovery in household consumption (following a peak in inflation in
Q1 2027). At the same time, external demand is expected to remain
strong, but its contribution to growth would be lower than in 2026.
This is because the stronger growth in domestic demand would stimu-

late imports to a greater extent.
INDUSTRY IS BENEFITING FROM AI INVESTMENT AND INTRA-EUROPEAN
MOMENTUM
The business climate in industry improved significantly at the start of
the second half of the year (the manufacturing PMI has risen by 4 points
since May, reaching a four-year high in August). New export orders have
even reached their highest level since early 2022. These positive signs
put into perspective the risks associated with the summer’s climate

events (heatwaves and the historic drought on the Rhine), which dis -
rupted industrial activity and river transport.
Since early 2025, output in some sectors has rebounded, particularly in
the electronics sector (+6.4 %). Manufacturers of electrical equipment,
as well as IT and electronics firms, are benefiting in particular from
the wave of investment in data centres and defence. If this momentum
continues, it could offset some of the losses recorded in the automotive

and chemicals sectors, which have both seen the sharpest falls in out-
put since late 2017 (-12.8% and -19.4%, respectively).
The sustained increase in military spending on medical technologies
and robotics across Europe is driving a restructuring of the interme -
diate-goods sector. Companies exposed to struggling sectors are gra -
dually turning towards these new markets. However, this reorientation
is not fully offsetting the loss of historical markets.

The rebound in German industry is also being fuelled by European
demand. While exports to China (-12.7% YTD in July) and the United
States (-1.5%) have certainly continued to fall, exports to a number of
European Union countries have risen sharply, particularly in Southern
and Central Europe. This shift is benefiting machinery and transport
equipment (47.6% of goods exports, up 20.5% since January 2025) in
particular. We expect this momentum to continue in 2027, creating a

virtuous cycle that reinforces European growth.
A STAGNANT LABOUR MARKET
The situation in the German labour market has stabilised over the
past several quarters, but it is not yet showing signs of improvement.
The unemployment rate is shifting relatively little (4% in November;
+0.2 pp y/y) and remains below the Eurozone average (6.4%). Falling
employment in the manufacturing sector (-0.6% since Q4 2019) is offset
by an increase in the services sector (+0.9%). Nevertheless, the latest

business climate surveys suggest that the situation may stabilise. The
gradual retraining of the workforce in struggling sectors is helping to
meet some of the needs arising from new investment.
Generally speaking, in the longer term, pension reform should support
the labour supply. As a matter of fact, it will help to increase the num-
ber of workers gradually, notably through the phasing out of early reti-
rement at 63 and the indexing of the retirement age to life expectancy

from 2032 onwards.
GERMANY
Growth and inflation

EcoPerspectives / 3rd Quarter 2026 / September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
The bank
for a changing
world
Although the unemployment rate is set to remain low, wage growth
would remain limited, particularly in 2026, as negotiated wages will
offset inflation to a lesser extent than they will in 2027. The planned
increases in the minimum wage (+8% in 2026 and +5% in 2027, to
EUR 14.6 per hour), significant as they are, will have little knock-on

effect on the rest of the wage scale.
PURCHASING POWER CONTINUES TO BE SQUEEZED BY HIGH INFLATION
Wage purchasing power is expected to fall further in 2026, having
already lost 4 pp since 2022, before stabilising in 2027. The reduction
in income tax (by EUR 10 billion, or 0.2% of GDP), which will come into
force next January, is also expected to support the disposable income of
low- and middle-income households.
Inflation has been rising again since the start of the summer and is

expected to remain above 2% throughout our forecast horizon. It has
accelerated since June (+0.5 pp to +2.9% y/yin August) driven by a fur -
ther rise in energy prices (+6.7 pp to +9.4% y/y), while core inflation re-
mains stable at +2.6%. From spring 2027 onwards, the expected easing
in energy prices (particularly gas prices) should bring down inflation.
However, this decline will be limited by the inflationary effects of the
wider roll-out of investment plans, as well as by the expected rise in

food prices.
This inflation, which has been structurally higher since the onset of the
2022 energy shock, will weigh on household consumption. Furthermore,
German households’ propensity to save remains high by international
standards (with a net balance 15 points higher on average over the
last ten years, according to the European Commission), which is also
weighing on their consumption.
FISCAL STIMULUS CONTINUES
After recording a public deficit of -3% of GDP in 2025, we anticipate a

further deterioration in the public balance to -4% in 2026, then -4.2%
in 2027. The primary deficit is projected to reach -2.8% of GDP in 2026
(after -1.9% in 2025) before narrowing slightly to -2.7% in 2027. At the
same time, the interest burden is expected to rise gradually from 1.1%
of GDP in 2025 to 1.5% in 2027. This combination will result in a rapid
rise in the debt-to-GDP ratio, from 62.6% of GDP in 2025 to 66.1% in
2026 and then 69.7% in 2027, before approaching 85% by the end of the

decade.
The yield on the 10-year Bund rose above 3.5% on 14 September, its
highest level since 2007. In addition to the increase in issues linked to
extra-budgetary funds, inflationary pressures are also contributing to
this rise. A gradual easing of inflation next year would make a mode -
rate easing of sovereign yields possible in 2027. However, yields would
remain high, at 3.2% in Q4 2027, suggesting that the bulk of the rise is
structural.
At the same time, the government is continuing to implement its pro -

gramme of structural reforms, including measures to support business
investment, health-insurance reform, pension reform and an overhaul
of the tax system. A number of measures have come into force, par -
ticularly accelerated depreciation for business investment until 2027,
which will be followed by a reduction in corporation tax from 2028.
Administrative simplification is also set to lead to an overhaul of the
tax system from January 2027 1. The health-insurance reform has been

passed, while the pension reform is still under discussion, with expecta-
tions that it will be adopted by the end of the year.
1  The main measure will be a reduction of EUR 10 billion per year in income tax for individuals on low incomes, funded in part by an increase in taxes on high earners. The bill will
be submitted to Parliament in the autumn, before being implemented from 2027.
Article completed on 14 September 2026

Document 351: Insurance costs hit the brakes Insurance payment growth is cooling, but the relief consumers are seeing depends heavily on who and where they are. 2 min read

Source PDF: all report pdf/Insurance costs hit the brakes Insurance payment growth is cooling, but the relief consumers are seeing depends heavily on who and where they are. 2 min read.pdf

Date: 2026-10-01

### Page 1

13028742     1

Economy 
Insurance costs hit the brakes 
16 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Property insurance (e.g., auto, homeowners, renters) payment growth has slowed considerably, according to Bank of America 
payments data. Median payments rose just 1% year-over-year (YoY) in 2026, easing further from the 6% growth seen in 2025, 
after a roughly 12% increase in 2024, suggesting that many consumers are getting some relief from rising insurance payments. 
• However, the moderation is uneven across consumers. Bank of America payments data suggests that Gen Z continues to 
experience insurance payment growth of nearly 6%, while increases for other generations are closer to 1% to 2%. Payments 
also rose nearly 1.5% for higher-income households but edged lower for those with lower incomes. 
• Lower accident rates, easing vehicle prices, and softer major-purchase growth may be reducing cost pressures, but geography 
remains a major dividing line. Florida metros recorded declines of roughly 7% to 8%, while California and several Northeastern 
metros continued to see relatively strong increases.

Insurance payments stabilize after years of painful growth 
The burden of higher property (e.g., auto, renters, homeowners) insurance payments may be easing for some. Notably, while the 
average payment increased 3% year-over-year (YoY), the median payment was only up 1%, according to Bank of America 
payments data. This suggests that most consumers have only seen a small increase in their premiums, though there may be a 
few outliers (Exhibit 1).  
The latest data stands in contrast to just two years ago in 2024, when most consumers saw their insurance payments surge 
about 12% YoY, after a nearly 8% YoY increase in 2023.   
Exhibit 1: Insurance payments have increased only modestly through August of this year, with growth peaking in 2024 
Median and average insurance payments (September to August yearly, YoY%)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Insurance payments by age show a similar pattern, with much stronger growth in 2024 across most generations followed by a 
cooling since then (Exhibit 2). Gen Z is the outlier here, with their payments still growing at a healthy rate, increasing almost 6% 
YoY for both 2026 and 2025, after a 7% increase in 2024. Meanwhile, Millennials and Baby Boomers experienced a 2% YoY 
increase so far this year, with Gen X only seeing around a 1% rise.

Fewer accidents and lower vehicles prices may be driving insurance payments down 
But why might insurance payments be declining at all? One thing to keep in mind is that insurance companies often raise rates 
to cover events that occurred in previous years to recover costs, while adjusting for changing risks.  
For example, the crash rate – the number of vehicle crashes per one million miles driven – suggests that there was a sharp rise 
in accidents in 2021, according to the Department of Transportation (Exhibit 4). Additionally, new and used vehicle prices 
increased significantly after the pandemic, according to Consumer Price Index (CPI) data from the Bureau of Labor Statistics 
(BLS) (Exhibit 5). So, insurers were not only facing more vehicle replacements due to a higher crash rate, but a higher cost to 
replace vehicles as well.

Florida’s metros have seen the largest decline when compared to other major cities 
Examining different cities across the US also sheds light on insurance trends. Consumers in major California metros saw the 
largest increases in 2026, following near double digit growth in 2025, likely driven by widespread property damage from 
wildfires, according to BofA Global Research (Exhibit 7). Major coastal metros in the Northeast like Boston and New York also 
experienced sizable increases as well.  
Conversely, many cities throughout the Sun Belt like Houston, Nashville and Dallas saw flat or even declining insurance 
payments. BofA Global Research points to several reasons for these declines: part of the equation in this region is that the 
massive runup in the cost of property insurance and domestic migration to the region through 2024 has since eased or reversed 
course.   
Exhibit 7: California major metros have seen steady insurance payment growth, while Miami metros have seen declines 
Median insurance payments by core based statistical areas (September to August yearly, YoY%)

In Florida, legislation that went into effect in 2023, eventually made its way into insurance prices in 2025, leading to widespread 
declines in premiums. The state also has a “lookback period” of three years, where insurance profits can be deemed too high and 
a rebate is issued to consumers. It’s possible this rebate is factoring into declines this year, as some carriers only allow rebates 
upon renewals.  
The Sunshine State was a notable standout for declines, with Orlando, Tampa and Jacksonville each down around 8% YoY in 
2026, followed closely by Miami with a 7% decrease. Additionally, Bank of America payments data suggests that Jacksonville, 
Miami and Orlando all had median insurance payments above the national level in 2023, though their payments were fell below 
that in 2026 (Exhibit 8).  
Major metros in Texas also saw easing insurance payments relative to 2023, while Seattle, San Jose, and Riverside, CA are 
among the major metros that went from below the national median to above in the three-year period ending in 2026.

Methodology  
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any payments data represents aggregated spend from US Retail, Preferred, Small Business and Wealth Management clients with a deposit 
account or credit card. Aggregated spend include total credit card, debit card, ACH, wires, bill pay, business/peer-to-peer, cash, and checks.  
Any Small Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of America per Small 
Business client data represents activity spending from active Small Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit card and 
at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and generally defined as 
under $5mm in annual sales revenue.  
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
The differences between the total and per household card spending growth rate (if discussed) can be explained by the following reasons:  
1. Overall total card spending growth is partially boosted by the growth in the number of active cardholders in our sample. This could be due to 
an increasing customer base or inactive customers using their cards more frequently. 
2. Per household card spending growth only looks at households that complete at least five transactions with Bank of America cards in the 
month. Per household spending growth isolates impacts from a changing sample size, which could be unrelated to underlying economic 
momentum, and potential spending volatility from less active users. 
3. Overall total card spending includes small business card spending while per household card spending does not. 
4. Differences due to using processing dates (total card spending) versus transaction date (per household card spending). 
5. Other differences including household formations due to young adults moving in and out of their parent’s houses during COVID. 
Any household consumer deposit data based on Bank of America internal data is derived by anonymizing and aggregating data from Bank of 
America consumer deposit accounts in the US and analyzing that data at a highly aggregated level. Whenever median household savings and 
checking balances are quoted, the data is based on a fixed cohort of households that had a consumer deposit account (checking and/or savings 
account) for all months from January 2019 through the most current month of data shown.  
Bank of America aggregated credit/debit card spending per household includes spending from active US households only. Only consumer card 
holders making a minimum of five transactions a month are included in the dataset. Spending from corporate cards are excluded. Data 
regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Insurance payments include renters, auto or home, but do not include insurance escrowed into mortgage payments or deducted from a 
paycheck.  
We consider a measure of services necessity spending that includes but is not limited to groceries, utilities, and gasoline. Discretionary services 
includes but is not limited to charitable donations, leisure travel, entertainment, and professional/consumer services. Discretionary retail 
includes but is not limited to general merchandise, miscellaneous, clothing, electronics and furniture. It excludes categories like groceries and 
gasoline. Holiday spending is defined as items in which spending in the November-December period is usually at least 20% of total annual 
spending on the category.  Value and premium grocers are determined judgmentally on a proprietary analysis of merchants by equity analysts. 
The “more discretionary” retail category includes furniture, electronics, building materials, online marketplaces, clothing retailers and 
miscellaneous stores; while “partly discretionary” retail contains auto parts and services, health and personal care retailers, general merchandise 
and “mostly necessity”  retail contains gas and groceries. 
Lower, middle and higher household income cuts in Bank of America credit and debit card spending per household, and consumer deposit 
account data are based on quantitative estimates of each households’ income. These quantitative estimates are bucketed according to terciles, 
with a third of households placed in each tercile periodically. The lowest tercile represents ‘lower income’, the middle tercile represents ‘middle 
income’ and the highest tercile ‘higher income’. The income thresholds between these terciles will move over time, reflecting any number of 
factors that impact income, including general wage inflation, changes in social security payments and individual households’ income. The 
income and tercile in which a household is categorised are periodically re-assessed.  
Generations, if discussed, are defined as follows: Gen Z, born after 1995; Younger Millennials: born between 1989-1995; Older Millennials: born 
between 1978-1988; Gen Xers: born between 1965-1977; Baby Boomer: 1946-1964; Traditionalists: pre-1946. 
Any reference to card spending per household on gasoline includes all purchases at gasoline stations and might include purchases of non-gas 
items. 
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.
